## Instruction Priority

Superpowers skills override default system prompt behavior, but **user instructions always take precedence**:

1. **User's explicit instructions** (CLAUDE.md, GEMINI.md, AGENTS.md, direct requests) — highest priority
2. **Superpowers skills** — override default system behavior where they conflict
3. **Default system prompt** — lowest priority

If CLAUDE.md, GEMINI.md, or AGENTS.md says "don't use TDD" and a skill says "always use TDD," follow the user's instructions. The user is in control.

## How to Access Skills

**In Claude Code:** Use the `Skill` tool. When you invoke a skill, its content is loaded and presented to you—follow it directly. Never use the Read tool on skill files.

**In Copilot CLI:** Use the `skill` tool. Skills are auto-discovered from installed plugins. The `skill` tool works the same as Claude Code's `Skill` tool.

**In Gemini CLI:** Skills activate via the `activate_skill` tool. Gemini loads skill metadata at session start and activates the full content on demand.

**In other environments:** Check your platform's documentation for how skills are loaded.

## Platform Adaptation

Skills use Claude Code tool names. Non-CC platforms: see `references/copilot-tools.md` (Copilot CLI), `references/codex-tools.md` (Codex) for tool equivalents. Gemini CLI users get the tool mapping loaded automatically via GEMINI.md.

# Using Skills

## The Rule

**Invoke relevant or requested skills BEFORE any response or action.** Even a 1% chance a skill might apply means that you should invoke the skill to check. If an invoked skill turns out to be wrong for the situation, you don't need to use it.

```dot
digraph skill_flow {
    "User message received" [shape=doublecircle];
    "About to EnterPlanMode?" [shape=doublecircle];
    "Already brainstormed?" [shape=diamond];
    "Invoke brainstorming skill" [shape=box];
    "Might any skill apply?" [shape=diamond];
    "Invoke Skill tool" [shape=box];
    "Announce: 'Using [skill] to [purpose]'" [shape=box];
    "Has checklist?" [shape=diamond];
    "Create TodoWrite todo per item" [shape=box];
    "Follow skill exactly" [shape=box];
    "Respond (including clarifications)" [shape=doublecircle];

    "About to EnterPlanMode?" -> "Already brainstormed?";
    "Already brainstormed?" -> "Invoke brainstorming skill" [label="no"];
    "Already brainstormed?" -> "Might any skill apply?" [label="yes"];
    "Invoke brainstorming skill" -> "Might any skill apply?";

    "User message received" -> "Might any skill apply?";
    "Might any skill apply?" -> "Invoke Skill tool" [label="yes, even 1%"];
    "Might any skill apply?" -> "Respond (including clarifications)" [label="definitely not"];
    "Invoke Skill tool" -> "Announce: 'Using [skill] to [purpose]'";
    "Announce: 'Using [skill] to [purpose]'" -> "Has checklist?";
    "Has checklist?" -> "Create TodoWrite todo per item" [label="yes"];
    "Has checklist?" -> "Follow skill exactly" [label="no"];
    "Create TodoWrite todo per item" -> "Follow skill exactly";
}
```

## Red Flags

These thoughts mean STOP—you're rationalizing:

| Thought | Reality |
|---------|---------|
| "This is just a simple question" | Questions are tasks. Check for skills. |
| "I need more context first" | Skill check comes BEFORE clarifying questions. |
| "Let me explore the codebase first" | Skills tell you HOW to explore. Check first. |
| "I can check git/files quickly" | Files lack conversation context. Check for skills. |
| "Let me gather information first" | Skills tell you HOW to gather information. |
| "This doesn't need a formal skill" | If a skill exists, use it. |
| "I remember this skill" | Skills evolve. Read current version. |
| "This doesn't count as a task" | Action = task. Check for skills. |
| "The skill is overkill" | Simple things become complex. Use it. |
| "I'll just do this one thing first" | Check BEFORE doing anything. |
| "This feels productive" | Undisciplined action wastes time. Skills prevent this. |
| "I know what that means" | Knowing the concept ≠ using the skill. Invoke it. |

## Skill Priority

When multiple skills could apply, use this order:

1. **Process skills first** (brainstorming, debugging) - these determine HOW to approach the task
2. **Implementation skills second** (frontend-design, mcp-builder) - these guide execution

"Let's build X" → brainstorming first, then implementation skills.
"Fix this bug" → debugging first, then domain-specific skills.

## Skill Types

**Rigid** (TDD, debugging): Follow exactly. Don't adapt away discipline.

**Flexible** (patterns): Adapt principles to context.

The skill itself tells you which.

## User Instructions

Instructions say WHAT, not HOW. "Add X" or "Fix Y" doesn't mean skip workflows.

# Networking: io_uring

## Goal
A Linux PLAINTEXT listener that runs through Netty's `IoUringEventLoopGroup` behind Kafka's existing `Selectable` interface, with a graceful NIO fallback on non-Linux and on other security protocols. Zero changes under `clients/`.

## Constraints (do not violate)
- **Do not modify anything under `clients/`.** The selector lives broker-side; clients connect as normal.
- v1: **PLAINTEXT only.** SSL / SASL_PLAINTEXT / SASL_SSL fall back to NIO.
- Non-Linux: graceful NIO fallback (compile-time detection or runtime probe, not a crash).
- Use **Netty** for everything. Do **not** build a separate Panama FFI path — the deck team tried it and abandoned it.
- Do **not** reach for virtual threads. They're empirically worse above 50 connections (per-task work ~1 μs is below scheduling cost). The bottleneck is kernel-side, not userspace.

## Minimum viable outcome
1. Wrap `IoUringEventLoopGroup` to satisfy Kafka's `Selectable` interface.
2. Bridge Netty's event loop ↔ Kafka's Processor thread via a `ConcurrentLinkedQueue` (one bridge thread per Processor).
3. Broker config `socket.selector.implementation = io_uring | nio | auto` (default `auto`: io_uring on Linux + PLAINTEXT, NIO otherwise).
4. PLAINTEXT 10k-connection benchmark vs the NIO baseline. Numbers in a short note in the worktree — direction matters more than chasing the deck's exact percentages.

## Stretch (only after the minimum lands)
- SSL, SASL_PLAINTEXT, SASL_SSL via Netty SSL/SASL handlers.
- TLS offload tuning.
- Per-Processor pinning and event-loop sizing knobs.

## Careful
- Netty's event loop runs on its own thread; Kafka's Processor expects to drive I/O from its own thread. Without the bridge queue, you deadlock.
- Measure before optimising. Userspace cleverness made things worse for the deck team — the work is in the kernel.

## Lessons already known (don't rediscover)
- Two selector implementations doubles the bug surface. One backend (Netty), one fallback (NIO). Stop there.
- Virtual threads are a trap at this granularity. Skip them.

## Acceptance criteria
- Linux platform detection is automatic at initialisation; the io_uring selector throws with a clear error message if invoked on a non-Linux OS or a kernel without io_uring support. Selection of NIO vs io_uring happens at broker startup, not per-request.
- PLAINTEXT is the primary supported security protocol on the io_uring path. SSL, SASL_PLAINTEXT, and SASL_SSL are supported via the Netty handler pipeline; protocols not yet verified must fall back to NIO rather than fail.
- Cross-thread signalling between the Netty event loop and the Kafka Processor thread uses ConcurrentLinkedQueues — at minimum one for completed-receive events and one for disconnect events. There is one event-loop + queue pair per Selectable instance (per Processor).
- Virtual threads are not used anywhere in the completion-processing path.
- Idle-connection expiry on the io_uring path matches the NIO baseline: the same `connectionsMaxIdleNanos` parameter is respected, `ChannelState.EXPIRED` is emitted when the threshold is exceeded, and no busy-loops or spurious wakeups occur.
- Mute / unmute semantics match NIO: muting suppresses read-event delivery from the event loop; unmuting resumes delivery; toggling cycles cleanly without leaks or deadlocks.

## Functional test scenarios
- On a Linux host with io_uring support, the broker starts with the io_uring selector active; a PLAINTEXT client connects, registers, and exchanges a size-prefixed message round-trip; the selector reports completed sends and completed receives correctly; no spurious disconnects or idle expirations occur during steady-state traffic.
- A multi-protocol harness exercises PLAINTEXT, SSL, SASL_PLAINTEXT, and SASL_SSL against the io_uring backend on Linux; all handshakes complete and payloads transit correctly (or unsupported combinations fall back to NIO without crashing).
- On macOS or Windows (or any environment without io_uring), broker startup either throws with a clear unsupported-platform message or selects NIO automatically; no warning spam, no crash, no degraded behaviour.
- A short idle-timeout configuration (e.g., 100 ms) is applied to multiple registered connections; with no traffic, only the idle connections appear in `selector.disconnected()`; active connections that exchanged traffic remain connected.
- A connection is muted; the peer sends data; the next selector poll returns no read events. The connection is then unmuted; the next poll exposes the data. Mute / unmute cycles repeatedly without resource leaks.

## When stuck (escape hatch)
If you find yourself looping on the same dead-end — the same error reappearing, the same refactor reverted, no measurable progress over several attempts — the order of recourse is fixed:

1. **First, ask Codex** (OpenAI's app) for a second opinion on the specific blocker. Frame the question sharply: what you tried, what went wrong, what you suspect. Take its answer seriously and try the direction it suggests.
2. **If still stuck after Codex, ask Gemini** (Google's app) for an independent third opinion. Compare the two — divergence is informative; convergence is reassuring. Try the most credible direction.
3. **Only if you remain stuck after both Codex and Gemini**, you may consult `/home/florent/ivy-trunk` read-only **for inspiration only**. Read enough to understand the shape of a solution, then close the file and write your own from scratch in this repo's idiom. **No copy-paste. No transliteration. No "I'll just adapt this block."**

The commit that follows an ivy-trunk consultation must include in its message a one-liner of the form: `Stuck in local minimum on <one-line description>; Codex suggested <X>; Gemini suggested <Y>; consulted ivy-trunk for inspiration; took away <one-sentence insight>; moved on.` If you cannot honestly write that sentence, you copied — revert and try again. The escape hatch exists to break unproductive loops, not to import an implementation.

## Working mode: full autonomy with sub-agent verification
You must drive this project to completion in **total autonomy**. No human babysitting, no mid-flight permission requests, no "should I continue?" check-ins. Plan, decide, implement, test, iterate, ship. The acceptance criteria and functional test scenarios above are your contract — done means they all pass.

**At the start of every task, fan out a fleet of sub-agents to figure out HOW to do it.** Tailor the fleet to the task — e.g., one sub-agent to map the affected code paths, one to identify the right Kafka APIs and existing patterns to reuse, one to surface edge cases and prior art in this repo, one to enumerate known pitfalls and lessons-already-known. Synthesize their findings before writing your first line of implementation code; do not skip this step because the task "looks simple."

**After every major phase** (initial planning closed; minimum viable outcome reached; each stretch item considered; before merge), fan out another fleet of sub-agents to **audit what happened**: spec compliance against the acceptance criteria and functional test scenarios above, test coverage, regressions in adjacent code, performance impact, edge cases missed, security/authorisation correctness. **Two slots in this audit fleet are mandatory**: one sub-agent consults **Codex** (OpenAI's app), one sub-agent consults **Gemini** (Google's app). Feed each the diff and a sharp question. Take their feedback seriously — integrate what improves the work, push back in writing on what doesn't, never silently ignore.

You may also consult Codex on demand mid-phase when choosing between two non-trivial design options.

Record each LLM consultation briefly in the commit body: `Codex: asked <one-line question>; took <one-line takeaway>. Gemini: asked <one-line question>; took <one-line takeaway>.` Never skip the audit because you "feel done."

commit a9138aabdcc2e40f8a8c9d4bd767953c76f13d72
Author: Florent Ramiere <florent@ramiere.com>
Date:   Mon May 18 12:30:30 2026 +0200

    io_uring: route InvalidReceiveException through LOCAL_CLOSE instead of escaping poll()
    
    Codex v5 BLOCKER 2.
    
    Symptom
    -------
    A client sending a malformed wire frame (negative size prefix, or a size
    larger than `socket.request.max.bytes`) makes KafkaChannel.read() throw
    InvalidReceiveException. That class extends KafkaException -> RuntimeException
    -- it is NOT an IOException.
    
    IoUringSelector.poll() previously caught only IOException in three places:
    
      - the step-2 read loop
      - the step-2 write loop
      - drainClosingChannels final-read
      - drainPendingDisconnects FIN drain
    
    Consequence: InvalidReceiveException would escape poll() to Processor.poll(),
    which catches Throwable but is structured around the assumption that selector
    output (disconnected, completedReceives) already reflects the failure. The
    channel was never closed via enqueueClose, so:
    
      - the connection quota was never decremented;
      - the channel stayed in `channels` until idle expiry;
      - unread Netty ByteBufs stayed pinned in the transport's inbound queue;
      - the pattern under a deliberate attacker is a slow direct-memory leak.
    
    Fix
    ---
    Broaden the four `catch (IOException e)` blocks to `catch (Exception e)`.
    This matches NIO Selector.pollSelectionKeys (clients/.../Selector.java:609)
    which catches Exception at the outer level. RuntimeException-class failures
    from KafkaChannel.read()/write() now route through the same
    enqueueClose(LOCAL_CLOSE) / enqueueClose(FAILED_SEND) path as IOExceptions:
    disconnected is populated in the same poll, the connection quota is
    decremented by Processor.processDisconnected on the next round, and the
    Netty channel + buffers are released.
    
    Regression test
    ---------------
    IoUringSelectorTest.malformedFrameSizeClosesTheChannelRatherThanEscapingPoll
    exercises both NetworkReceive guards:
    
      1. size = -1 (negative-size guard)
      2. size = MAX_RECEIVE + 1 (oversized-size guard)
    
    For each, the test asserts poll() does NOT throw and that the channel
    surfaces in disconnected() with ChannelState.LOCAL_CLOSE on the read poll.
    
    Also moves a misplaced Javadoc block that described drainPendingDisconnects
    back above its own method (it had been positioned above drainClosingChannels).
    
    Co-Authored-By: Claude Opus 4.7 <noreply@anthropic.com>

@@ -379,7 +379,15 @@ public final class IoUringSelector implements BrokerSelector {
                         receivesThisPoll.add(channel.id());
                         madeProgress = true;
                     }
-                } catch (IOException e) {
+                } catch (Exception e) {
+                    // Catch Exception, not just IOException: KafkaChannel.read() declares
+                    // throws IOException, but NetworkReceive throws InvalidReceiveException
+                    // (a KafkaException -> RuntimeException) when the wire reports a negative
+                    // size or a size > socket.request.max.bytes. Catching only IOException
+                    // lets that escape poll() so Processor.poll() never sees the failure,
+                    // the channel is never closed, the connection quota is never decremented,
+                    // and the unread Netty ByteBufs accumulate until idle expiry or direct-

commit 65e29cd5704b9fc0119574f66aecc4b6c91149a6
Author: Florent Ramiere <florent@ramiere.com>
Date:   Mon May 18 12:40:36 2026 +0200

    io_uring: gate writes when Netty outboundBuffer hits high water mark
    
    BLOCKER (Codex v5 audit, blocker 3): IoUringTransportLayer.write()
    unconditionally allocates a pooled directBuffer per call and hands it
    to nettyChannel.writeAndFlush() without checking whether Netty's
    outbound queue is already past its high water mark. A slow peer that
    stops draining a 50 MB FetchResponse leaves the bytes stranded in
    Netty's outboundBuffer; subsequent writes keep allocating direct memory
    for that channel until the broker OOMs.
    
    The per-channel single-Send-in-flight contract (KafkaChannel.send !=
    null guards future sends until the current one drains) was what we
    were leaning on to bound this — but a single in-flight Send is enough
    to blow up direct memory when the Send itself is large and the peer
    is slow.
    
    Fix: gate write() on nettyChannel.isWritable(). When false, return 0
    so ByteBufferSend.writeTo keeps its source ByteBuffers intact and the
    next Selector poll's write step retries. Netty's
    onWritabilityChanged() fires the moment the outbound buffer drains
    below the low water mark and wakes the poll thread, so the retry
    isn't blocked on the idle timeout. This mirrors NIO write() returning
    0 when SO_SNDBUF is saturated.
    
    The gate is conditioned on isActive() so closed/disconnected channels
    don't get silently swallowed — for those, isWritable() is also false
    but we WANT writeAndFlush to proceed so its promise fails and
    surfaces the error through asyncWriteFailure (the path tested by
    asyncWriteFailureSurfacesAsIoExceptionOnNextWrite and
    asyncWriteFailureSurfacesThroughVectoredWriteEvenWhenAllBuffersEmpty).
    Returning 0 on a dead channel would deadlock ByteBufferSend.writeTo
    forever.
    
    Test uses outboundBuffer().setUserDefinedWritability(1, false) to
    force isWritable() to false directly — isolates the test to the gate
    logic rather than depending on EmbeddedChannel's water-mark
    accounting (which, for writeAndFlush output that we pop via
    readOutbound, doesn't accumulate against the mark the way a real
    SocketChannel would).
    
    Co-Authored-By: Claude Opus 4.7 <noreply@anthropic.com>

         int remaining = src.remaining();
         if (remaining == 0) return 0;
+        // Backpressure: if Netty's outbound buffer has exceeded the high water mark,
+        // refuse to queue more bytes. Returning 0 makes ByteBufferSend.writeTo keep
+        // its source ByteBuffers intact, so the next Selector poll's write step will
+        // re-attempt — and onWritabilityChanged() will wake the poll the moment the
+        // buffer drains below the low water mark. Without this gate, each write()
+        // unconditionally allocates a fresh direct ByteBuf and hands it to Netty's
+        // unbounded outboundBuffer; a slow peer would let direct memory grow until
+        // the broker OOMs. This is the io_uring analog of NIO write() returning 0
+        // when the kernel's SO_SNDBUF is saturated.
+        //
+        // Restrict the gate to healthy channels (isActive() = open + connected).
+        // For a closed/disconnected channel, isWritable() is also false — but we
+        // WANT those writes to proceed so writeAndFlush's promise fails and
+        // surfaces the error through asyncWriteFailure. Returning 0 here on a
+        // dead channel would silently swallow the error: ByteBufferSend.writeTo
+        // would loop forever waiting for the channel to become writable again,
+        // which it never will, while the Send is silently considered "in flight".
+        if (nettyChannel.isActive() && !nettyChannel.isWritable()) {
+            return 0;
+        }
         // Use a pooled direct buffer so io_uring can submit the bytes without a heap-to-
         // direct intermediate copy. The buffer is released by Netty after the channel has
         // flushed it; we only own the writeAndFlush completion listener.

commit 586e8a83fd6bccdc089c246737866c80bb4cc7c3
Author: Florent Ramiere <florent@ramiere.com>
Date:   Mon May 18 12:45:39 2026 +0200

    io_uring: bound pendingAccepts queue to prevent unbounded growth
    
    BLOCKER (Codex v5 audit, blocker 4): IoUringSelector.pendingAccepts is
    a ConcurrentLinkedQueue with no upper bound. Netty's io_uring event
    loop calls onAccept() for every accepted connection; each call builds
    a full KafkaChannel stack (IoUringTransportLayer, Authenticator,
    ChannelMetadataRegistry, plus a slot in nettyChannels) and offers it
    to the queue. If the Processor stalls — request-handler backpressure,
    GC pause, slow downstream — the queue grows indefinitely and the
    broker OOMs.
    
    The NIO analog (SocketServer.Acceptor) uses a bounded
    ArrayBlockingQueue(connectionQueueSize) and blocks the Acceptor on
    put() when full, propagating backpressure through to the OS accept
    backlog. We can't block — the Netty event loop is shared across this
    listener's child channels and freezing it would stall every accepted
    connection, not just the new ones — so when the cap is hit we close
    the freshly accepted netty channel synchronously. The client sees a
    TCP RST/FIN; the OS backlog absorbs the pressure.
    
    Cap is 20 per selector (matches NIO's hardcoded per-Processor cap).
    Total system cap is N * 20 across N selectors, same as NIO.
    
    Implementation detail: a separate AtomicInteger tracks queue depth.
    ConcurrentLinkedQueue.size() walks the chain and would race the
    producer/consumer pointers under load. The counter is incremented
    after offer() (so we never report "full" when nothing is enqueued
    yet), decremented when the Processor drains an entry (poll step 1)
    and when the selector closes (close() drain).
    
    Co-Authored-By: Claude Opus 4.7 <noreply@anthropic.com>

commit 6e5b5a9e431905f6803b0ad0a278d356efcaf270
Author: Florent Ramiere <florent@ramiere.com>
Date:   Mon May 18 13:07:07 2026 +0200

    io_uring: recover self-muted channels when MemoryPool releases pressure
    
    v6 audit BLOCKER from the Claude-side selector review: the io_uring selector
    had no equivalent of NIO Selector's MemoryPool recovery loop. Under sustained
    queued.max.bytes pressure the io_uring listener could deadlock indefinitely.
    
    THE BUG
    
    KafkaChannel.read() self-mutes when memoryPool.tryAllocate(payloadSize)
    returns null (clients/.../KafkaChannel.java:414-417):
    
        if (this.receive.requiredMemoryAmountKnown()
                && !this.receive.memoryAllocated()
                && isInMutableState()) {
            // pool must be out of memory, mute ourselves.
            mute();
        }
    
    This is the BROKER-SIDE memory backpressure path: when SimpleMemoryPool is
    bounded by queued.max.bytes and the request-handling pipeline can't drain
    fast enough, KafkaChannel mutes itself so the kernel stops shipping more
    bytes through Netty. autoRead flips to false. Bytes stop arriving.
    
    NIO Selector.poll() recovers from this by walking the channel map every
    poll (clients/.../Selector.java:457-466):
    
        // Add to completedReceives after closing expired channels to avoid
        // their inclusion in `completedReceives`
        addToCompletedReceives();
        if (dataInBuffers) {
            ...
        }
    
        // we read responses from queues if there's any. If a memory pool
        // is in use & memory is low, we may now be able to free up some memory.
        if (memoryPool.isOutOfMemory() && !channelsWithBufferedData.isEmpty()) {
            ...
        }
    
        if (!memoryPool.isOutOfMemory() && outOfMemory) {
            // we were out of memory previously and now we're not, so unmute
            // any explicitly muted channels
            log.trace("Broker no longer low on memory - unmuting incoming sockets");
            for (KafkaChannel channel : channels.values()) {
                if (channel.isInMutableState() &&
                    !explicitlyMutedChannels.contains(channel)) {
                    channel.maybeUnmute();
                }
            }
            outOfMemory = false;
        }
    
    IoUringSelector had nothing equivalent. Once self-muted, a channel only
    recovered via idle expiry (default ~10 min), or never if traffic kept
    re-stamping lastActiveNanos. In a real broker under queued.max.bytes
    saturation, every channel that hit the self-mute path would wedge until
    expiry — the listener effectively dies.
    
    THE FIX
    
    1.  Add explicitlyMutedChannels Set<KafkaChannel>, mirroring NIO's storage
        at clients/.../Selector.java:108. Tracks channels the OPERATOR muted
        via mute(id)/muteAll() — distinct from channels that self-muted
        because the pool was dry.
    
    2.  Add outOfMemory boolean, set after a read step observes a channel
        transition from not-muted to muted while NOT in explicitlyMutedChannels.
        The only path that could have done that is KafkaChannel's internal
        self-mute. Mirrors NIO Selector.attemptRead lines 691-692.
    
    3.  Extract recoverFromMemoryPressure() helper, called at the top of
        poll(). When memoryPool.isOutOfMemory() reports false AND outOfMemory
        is set, walk channels and call maybeUnmute() — but skip channels in
        explicitlyMutedChannels so request-pipeline throttling (RESPONSE_QUEUED
        → RESPONSE_SENT) is not bypassed.
    
    4.  Keep explicitlyMutedChannels consistent on every channel-lifecycle
        transition: mute()/unmute() add/remove on success, muteAll/unmuteAll
        do the bulk equivalent, idle-expiry and enqueueClose and
        drainClosingChannels and close()/close(id) remove on teardown for
        both the live channels map and the closingChannels deferred-teardown
        map. Without these cleanups the set would slowly leak KafkaChannel
        references and pin them past their useful lifetime.
    
    The helper extraction was forced by checkstyle: inlining the recovery loop
    in poll() pushed it past the 170-line MethodLength limit. Extracting also
    makes the recovery surface easier to test independently if we ever want to.
    
    TESTING
    
    Two regression tests in IoUringSelectorTest:
    
    selfMutedChannelUnmutesOnceMemoryPoolRecovers
      Drains a SimpleMemoryPool(64), drives a framed payload through onRead,
      asserts the channel self-muted (autoRead off, no completedReceive). Then
      releases the pool and asserts the next poll unmutes the channel AND
      surfaces the previously-blocked completedReceive (the size header was
      already buffered; recovery lets the retry tryAllocate succeed).
    
    recoveryDoesNotUnmuteOperatorMutedChannels
      Saturates the pool, then operator-mutes one channel via selector.mute(id)
      while another self-mutes from the memory pressure. After pool release,
      asserts the self-muted channel is unmuted but the operator-muted one
      stays muted — the explicitlyMutedChannels gate must not let request-
      pipeline throttling be silently bypassed by memory recovery.
    
    Without these two cases, a future refactor could collapse the set into a
    flat "self-mute counter" and break the operator/self-mute distinction
    without anything failing.
    
    REFERENCES
    
      - NIO Selector recovery loop:           clients/.../Selector.java:457-466
      - NIO Selector attemptRead detection:   clients/.../Selector.java:691-692
      - NIO Selector explicitlyMutedChannels: clients/.../Selector.java:108, 750, 762-763
      - KafkaChannel self-mute trigger:       clients/.../KafkaChannel.java:407-419
      - KafkaChannel.isInMutableState:        clients/.../KafkaChannel.java:343-352
    
    Co-Authored-By: Claude Opus 4.7 <noreply@anthropic.com>

commit 30e2e2d372b3b183f0168c324866ef98ee242821
Author: Florent Ramiere <florent@ramiere.com>
Date:   Mon May 18 13:32:05 2026 +0200

    io_uring: drain self-muted closing channels (NIO line 702 parity)
    
    drainClosingChannels() in IoUringSelector previously short-circuited on
    the broader channel.isMuted(), which captures BOTH operator-driven mutes
    (RESPONSE_QUEUED, throttling) and self-mutes triggered when
    memoryPool.tryAllocate returns null inside KafkaChannel.read.
    
    The NIO baseline at clients/.../Selector.java:702 only short-circuits on
    the narrower explicitlyMutedChannels.contains(channel) predicate. Self-
    muted closing channels MUST still attempt to drain, otherwise a peer FIN
    arriving while queued.max.bytes is saturated strands the channel here
    forever: drain skips it on isMuted(), and recoverFromMemoryPressure()
    only walks channels.values() — never closingChannels.
    
    Failure mode without this fix:
      1. KafkaChannel.read() under MemoryPool pressure reads the size header,
         tryAllocate(payloadSize) returns null, channel self-mutes.
      2. Peer FINs. drainPendingDisconnects routes the channel to
         closingChannels (the !channel.isMuted() guard at line 687 skips the
         inline FIN-drain, by design).
      3. Every subsequent poll: drainClosingChannels sees channel.isMuted()
         and sets keepClosing=true. recoverFromMemoryPressure() does NOT
         touch closingChannels even when the pool refills.
      4. Result: connection-quota slot leaks indefinitely, queued ByteBufs
         and the KafkaChannel itself are retained until process death,
         PLAINTEXT listener slowly bleeds capacity under sustained load.
    
    Fix: narrow the short-circuit to explicitlyMutedChannels.contains() so
    self-muted closing channels still get channel.read() attempts in the
    drain loop. If memory has recovered, the read drains the buffered frame
    and surfaces a completedReceive. If memory is still pressed, no progress
    is made, keepClosing stays false, and the channel is evicted with
    LOCAL_CLOSE — recovering the quota slot and releasing the ByteBufs.
    
    Tests:
      - selfMutedClosingChannelDrainsRatherThanStickingForever: drains pool,
        drives a framed payload to trigger self-mute, FINs, polls twice
        (one for drainPendingDisconnects, one for drainClosingChannels),
        asserts the channel is evicted with LOCAL_CLOSE.
      - operatorMutedClosingChannelStaysUntilProcessorUnmutes: counterpart
        test that an operator-muted closing channel (in explicitlyMutedChannels)
        is still preserved across polls — preserves the throttling /
        RESPONSE_QUEUED handshake invariant.
    
    Found by Codex v7 audit on the post-v6 state (BLOCKER 3 of 3).
    
    Co-Authored-By: Claude Opus 4.7 <noreply@anthropic.com>

commit e33cd3bee964b017b15989f7469d1a6f9b34fd25
Author: Florent Ramiere <florent@ramiere.com>
Date:   Mon May 18 13:43:18 2026 +0200

    io_uring: bound transport-layer direct memory under DoS (inbound + outbound)
    
    Codex v7 audit flagged two complementary direct-memory exhaustion paths
    in IoUringTransportLayer. Both share the same shape — a single channel
    can spike enough direct memory before any existing backpressure check
    fires that the broker OOMs under load. Both fixed in one commit because
    they share the same constants/test scaffold.
    
    BLOCKER 1 (inbound): offerInbound queued ByteBufs without any byte/count
    bound while Netty AUTO_READ remained on. A fast/abusive PLAINTEXT client
    (or simply a slow Processor) lets ByteBufs accumulate without limit in
    the transport's inbound ConcurrentLinkedQueue. Direct-memory OOM trips
    before KafkaChannel's MemoryPool-backed queued.max.bytes throttle fires
    — that throttle only governs the destination NetworkReceive buffer, not
    the raw bytes still riding in this queue waiting to be drained.
    
      Fix: track per-channel inboundBytes (AtomicLong, event-loop writer +
      Processor reader). When offerInbound crosses INBOUND_HIGH_WATERMARK
      (1 MiB), flip Netty autoRead OFF so the kernel applies TCP-level
      backpressure to the peer. When read() drains below INBOUND_LOW (256
      KiB), flip autoRead back on — but ONLY on an unmuted channel
      (operator-mute or memory-pool self-mute must keep autoRead off
      regardless of queue depth, otherwise the request-pipeline throttle
      is bypassed). The HIGH-LOW gap prevents flag oscillation under a
      steady-state load near a single threshold.
    
    BLOCKER 2 (outbound): write(ByteBuffer) allocated a direct ByteBuf of
    size = src.remaining() in one shot, BEFORE the isWritable() check
    could flip. A 100 MiB Fetch response on an empty outbound queue
    allocated 100 MiB in a single call. Many concurrent slow consumers
    each spiked 100 MiB of direct memory — the isWritable() gate at the
    top of write() only blocks the NEXT call, never the offending one.
    
      Fix: cap each write to MAX_WRITE_CHUNK_BYTES (1 MiB) AND to Netty's
      bytesBeforeUnwritable() (remaining headroom under the high water
      mark, treating 0 as full backpressure latch). ByteBufferSend.writeTo
      re-enters on the next poll with the source ByteBuffer's remainder,
      mirroring how NIO returns partial progress when SO_SNDBUF saturates.
      Correctness for large responses is preserved; only the per-call
      allocation footprint changes.
    
    Why one commit: the inbound and outbound paths share neither code nor
    tests, but they share the same failure shape (single-channel direct-
    memory spike) and the same audit verdict (Codex v7 BLOCKERs 1 and 2
    of 3). Splitting would force the reader to mentally re-stitch them.
    
    Constants chosen:
      INBOUND_HIGH_WATERMARK_BYTES = 1 MiB
      INBOUND_LOW_WATERMARK_BYTES = 256 KiB
      MAX_WRITE_CHUNK_BYTES = 1 MiB
    The MAX_WRITE_CHUNK cap is deliberately larger than typical Kafka
    response chunks (Produce ack, Fetch slice) so steady-state traffic
    drains in a couple of poll iterations rather than thousands, while
    remaining small enough to bound per-call direct memory under DoS.
    
    Tests:
      - offerInboundFlipsAutoReadOffOnceHighWatermarkExceeded
      - readReEnablesAutoReadOnceBelowLowWatermark
      - readDoesNotReEnableAutoReadWhileMuted (throttling invariant)
      - writeChunksLargePayloadsToMaxWriteChunkBytes (raises Netty water
        marks so the chunking cap is exercised in isolation, not masked
        by Netty's default 32K/64K bytesBeforeUnwritable)
      - writeReturnsZeroOncePastHighWaterMarkRegardlessOfChunkSize
        (the chunking cap must NOT force a 1 MiB allocation through a
        backpressured channel — isWritable=false still latches first)
    
    Co-Authored-By: Claude Opus 4.7 <noreply@anthropic.com>

+    /**
+     * Hard upper bound on the number of bytes a single {@link #write(ByteBuffer)} call will
+     * allocate in one direct {@link ByteBuf}. Two failure modes are bounded by this cap:
+     *
+     * <ol>
+     *   <li><b>Direct-memory OOM under a single huge response.</b> A 100 MiB Fetch response
+     *       reaching a channel whose Netty outbound buffer is empty would otherwise allocate
+     *       a 100 MiB direct buffer in one shot — bypassing the high-water-mark gate, which
+     *       only flips after the queue is already past the limit. Capping each call to 1 MiB
+     *       keeps the per-call footprint bounded; the source ByteBuffer keeps its remainder
+     *       and ByteBufferSend re-enters on the next poll, mirroring how NIO returns partial
+     *       progress when {@code SO_SNDBUF} saturates.</li>
+     *   <li><b>Direct-memory exhaustion across many slow consumers.</b> Without the cap,
+     *       N concurrent in-flight big sends each allocate N * responseSize. Even with the
+     *       Netty watermark gate, a burst of slow consumers can collectively exceed direct
+     *       memory before any single channel trips the gate. The cap bounds the
+     *       worst-case per-call allocation, regardless of how many channels are writing.</li>
+     * </ol>
+     *
+     * <p>1 MiB is a deliberate balance: small enough to keep direct memory bounded under DoS,
+     * large enough that typical Kafka response sizes (Produce ack, Fetch chunk) drain in a
+     * couple of poll iterations rather than thousands.
+     */
+    static final int MAX_WRITE_CHUNK_BYTES = 1 << 20;

+    /**
+     * High-water mark for the per-channel inbound ByteBuf queue. When {@link #inboundBytes}
+     * exceeds this, {@link #offerInbound(ByteBuf)} flips Netty {@code autoRead} off so the
+     * kernel applies TCP-level backpressure to the peer. Without this gate, a fast malicious
+     * PLAINTEXT client (or simply a slow Processor) lets ByteBufs accumulate unbounded in

+     * MemoryPool only governs the destination NetworkReceive buffer, not the raw bytes

+    static final int INBOUND_HIGH_WATERMARK_BYTES = 1 << 20;

+     */

commit 5cf28535da2a3a15a96bbd84ca905f9ea9e7e13c
Author: Florent Ramiere <florent@ramiere.com>
Date:   Mon May 18 14:02:06 2026 +0200

    io_uring: preserve vectored-write byte ordering on partial chunk
    
    Codex v8 audit flagged that IoUringTransportLayer.write(ByteBuffer[],
    int, int) continues iterating the source array even when an earlier
    buffer was only partially drained — e.g. when the per-call
    MAX_WRITE_CHUNK_BYTES cap (added in v7 BLOCKER 2, commit e33cd3bee9)
    truncates a large middle buffer. That violates GatheringByteChannel's
    strict-ordering contract: bytes from a later buffer in the vector get
    queued on the wire ahead of the unread tail of the earlier buffer.
    
    Failure scenario
      ByteBufferSend.writeTo hands the transport an array shaped like
      [header, payload, trailer] — Kafka Fetch responses follow exactly
      this layout. When the payload exceeds MAX_WRITE_CHUNK_BYTES, the
      per-buffer write returns just the first 1 MiB, leaves the rest on
      the source ByteBuffer for the next poll, AND the loop then writes
      the trailer. Wire output becomes
      [header][1 MiB of payload][trailer][payload tail on next poll] —
      the trailer ends up inside the record set, corrupting the response.
    
      This is a silent on-wire corruption bug. Tests that don't span the
      chunk cap (every existing test except this one) won't catch it.
    
    Fix
      Stop the loop as soon as the inner write(ByteBuffer) returns 0
      (backpressure) OR leaves bytes remaining in the source buffer
      (chunk-cap hit). The caller (ByteBufferSend) re-enters on the next
      poll with the same source array and the loop continues where it
      left off — exactly how a JDK SocketChannel.write(ByteBuffer[]) call
      recovers across short writes.
    
    Regression coverage
      - vectoredWriteStopsAtFirstPartialBufferToPreserveOrdering:
        [header(8), payload(MAX_WRITE_CHUNK + 1024), trailer(4)] →
        header drains fully, payload has exactly 1024 bytes left, trailer
        is UNTOUCHED. The trailer-untouched assertion is the load-bearing
        one — without the loop break, trailer.remaining() would be 0.
      - vectoredWriteStopsWhenInnerWriteReturnsZero:
        backpressured channel + [first(64), second(64)] → both buffers
        untouched, write returns 0. Zero-return path is distinct from
        partial-write path; both need the break.
    
    Why this is BLOCKER for v1
      The corruption only fires once a Send crosses MAX_WRITE_CHUNK_BYTES
      (1 MiB), so steady-state small Produce/Fetch traffic appears fine,
      but a single sufficiently large Fetch — exactly the workload a
      consumer with a large fetch.max.bytes drives — corrupts the
      response. PRODUCTION-grade demands strictly correct framing.
    
    Co-Authored-By: Claude Opus 4.7 <noreply@anthropic.com>

diff --git a/server/src/main/java/org/apache/kafka/network/iouring/IoUringTransportLayer.java b/server/src/main/java/org/apache/kafka/network/iouring/IoUringTransportLayer.java

         for (int i = 0; i < length; i++) {
             ByteBuffer src = srcs[offset + i];
             if (!src.hasRemaining()) continue;
-            total += write(src);
+            // GatheringByteChannel ordering: a partial write of {@code src} (because of
+            // {@link #MAX_WRITE_CHUNK_BYTES} or {@code bytesBeforeUnwritable} pressure) must
+            // halt the loop here. Continuing to a later buffer would interleave its bytes
+            // ahead of the remainder of {@code src}, scrambling on-wire framing for any
+            // multi-buffer {@link org.apache.kafka.common.network.ByteBufferSend} —
+            // notably Fetch responses whose Send is built as
+            // {@code [header, recordSet, trailer]}: a partial write of {@code recordSet}
+            // followed by writes from {@code trailer} would emit trailer bytes inside
+            // the record set. {@link java.nio.channels.SocketChannel#write(ByteBuffer[])}
+            // never reorders, and our impl must match. A zero return (backpressured)
+            // similarly aborts the loop so the caller re-enters on the next poll.
+            int wrote = write(src);
+            total += wrote;
+            if (wrote == 0 || src.hasRemaining()) {
+                break;
+            }
         }
         return total;
     }

commit 975a7f2f32f7b43deec54b2ac00251716f33b565
Author: Florent Ramiere <florent@ramiere.com>
Date:   Mon May 18 14:19:17 2026 +0200

    io_uring: cap connection-id index at Integer.MAX_VALUE + un-dangle Javadoc
    
    Round 9 audit (Codex gpt-5.5 xhigh) surfaced two BLOCKERs in
    IoUringSelector.java; both fixed in this commit.
    
    BLOCKER 1 — connection-id wraparound (line 327 of audit)
    =========================================================
    idGen was AtomicLong, but ServerConnectionId.fromString() parses the
    index segment via Integer.parseInt (clients/.../ServerConnectionId.java:108).
    After ~2.1B accepts on a long-running broker — reachable under connection
    churn or a deliberate attack — the index segment exceeds Integer.MAX_VALUE,
    fromString returns Optional.empty, and SocketServer.processDisconnected
    cannot decrement ConnectionQuotas. The slot leaks until restart, and the
    broker eventually refuses legitimate connections.
    
    NIO has had this right all along: SocketServer.scala line 1431-1432 wraps
    explicitly: `if (nextConnectionIndex == Int.MaxValue) 0 else +1`. This
    commit mirrors that pattern exactly:
    
      idGen is now AtomicInteger
      idGen.getAndUpdate(i -> i == Integer.MAX_VALUE ? 0 : i + 1)
    
    Regression test connectionIdWrapsAtIntegerMaxValue uses reflection to
    pre-seed idGen to Integer.MAX_VALUE, accepts two channels, and asserts:
      - first id parses, index() == Integer.MAX_VALUE
      - second id parses, index() == 0 (wrapped, matches NIO semantics)
    Without the wrap, the second assert would fail with
    NumberFormatException → Optional.empty from fromString.
    
    BLOCKER 2 — dangling Javadoc (line 547 of audit)
    =================================================
    A Javadoc block belonging to drainClosingChannels() had been inserted
    above recoverFromMemoryPressure() during an earlier edit. Under -Werror
    (which Kafka enables in CI), javac emits
      [dangling-doc-comments] documentation comment is not attached to any declaration
    and the :server:compileJava task fails — meaning the test suite cannot
    run at all in CI. Moved the block down to attach to drainClosingChannels.
    The recoverFromMemoryPressure Javadoc that was already in the right
    place is unchanged.
    
    Verification
    ============
    JAVA_HOME=/usr/lib/jvm/java-21-openjdk-amd64 ./gradlew -q :server:test \
      --tests org.apache.kafka.network.iouring.IoUringSelectorTest \
      --tests org.apache.kafka.network.iouring.IoUringTransportLayerTest
    BUILD SUCCESSFUL.
    
    Co-Authored-By: Claude Opus 4.7 <noreply@anthropic.com>

commit 808a8d9c86965345a0250fb11bec9e95a2c796b3
Author: Florent Ramiere <florent@ramiere.com>
Date:   Mon May 18 14:33:25 2026 +0200

    io_uring: wake selector from async writeAndFlush listener
    
    Round 10 audit (Codex gpt-5.5 xhigh) surfaced this as a production
    BLOCKER: the writeAndFlush completion listener decremented
    pendingWriteBytes and recorded asyncWriteFailure but did NOT wake the
    Processor's poll() Semaphore.
    
    Failure path
    ============
    1. Processor enqueues a small response (e.g. a 1 KB Fetch reply).
    2. Step-2 of poll() invokes channel.write() → transport.write() →
       nettyChannel.writeAndFlush(buf). The source ByteBuffer is fully
       drained, but pendingWriteBytes goes to {safeChunk}, so
       transport.hasPendingWrites() reports true and
       KafkaChannel.maybeCompleteSend() returns null.
    3. Step-2 records madeProgress=true (because written > 0), so poll()
       completes this iteration without sleeping.
    4. The next poll() iteration finds no new I/O work: source ByteBuffers
       are drained, so channel.write() returns 0 and madeProgress=false.
    5. poll() drops into the wait at IoUringSelector.java:546 and blocks
       on the wakeup Semaphore for up to timeoutMs (300 ms by default).
    6. The Netty event loop completes the writeAndFlush future a few µs
       later and our listener fires, decrementing pendingWriteBytes to 0.
       But the listener had no wakeup hook → the Processor stays asleep.
    7. onWritabilityChanged is NOT a substitute: it only fires when the
       outbound buffer crosses the high/low water marks. For a typical
       small response that never crosses, no wakeup ever arrives.
    8. Result: every small request/response pair incurs a ~300 ms latency
       penalty before completedSends fires and RESPONSE_SENT advances.
       Throughput collapses on small-message workloads.
    
    Why this wasn't caught by tests
    ================================
    EmbeddedChannel completes writeAndFlush futures SYNCHRONOUSLY on the
    calling thread, so the listener runs inline with write() and the
    Processor never goes to sleep on a stale pendingWriteBytes count. The
    real production code path (Netty IoUringSocketChannel on a separate
    event-loop thread) is the only one that exposes the stall.
    
    Fix
    ===
    Add an optional `Runnable writeWakeCallback` argument to
    IoUringTransportLayer. IoUringSelector passes `wakeup::release` at
    construction. The writeAndFlush listener calls it AFTER decrementing
    pendingWriteBytes (so the next poll sees hasPendingWrites=false) and
    AFTER any failure stash (so FAILED_SEND routing is not delayed by the
    full poll timeout either).
    
    The single-arg constructor remains for tests that don't care about
    wakeup semantics — it installs a no-op Runnable.
    
    Tests
    =====
    1. writeWakeCallbackFiresOnceForEachWriteAndFlushCompletion — verifies
       the listener path invokes the callback exactly once per write.
    2. writeWakeCallbackFiresOnAsyncWriteFailure — closes the channel,
       issues a write that completes exceptionally, asserts the wake
       callback still fires (so FAILED_SEND routing isn't delayed).
    
    Verification
    ============
    JAVA_HOME=/usr/lib/jvm/java-21-openjdk-amd64 ./gradlew -q :server:test \
      --tests org.apache.kafka.network.iouring.IoUringSelectorTest \
      --tests org.apache.kafka.network.iouring.IoUringTransportLayerTest
    BUILD SUCCESSFUL.
    
    Co-Authored-By: Claude Opus 4.7 <noreply@anthropic.com>

commit 03aff737deba28e22d4ef84db45494b4461881b6
Author: Florent Ramiere <florent@ramiere.com>
Date:   Mon May 18 14:47:08 2026 +0200

    io_uring: bind wildcard listener for blank/null host (PLAINTEXT://:9092)
    
    Round 11 audit (Codex gpt-5.5 xhigh) surfaced a startup-time BLOCKER:
    the io_uring processor bundle constructed the bind address as
    `new InetSocketAddress(endPoint.host, endPoint.port)` unconditionally.
    
    Failure scenario
    ================
    Kafka's SocketServerConfigs.listenerListToEndPoints stores wildcard
    listeners (`PLAINTEXT://:9092`) with `endPoint.host == null`. The
    two-arg InetSocketAddress constructor rejects a null hostname with
    `IllegalArgumentException: hostname can't be null`. Under the default
    config (`socket.selector.implementation=auto` + Linux + PLAINTEXT)
    the auto path picks io_uring, so a stock broker config of
    `listeners=PLAINTEXT://:9092` fails at Processor construction —
    before any client ever connects. An empty string would silently bind
    localhost instead of the wildcard, also diverging from NIO.
    
    NIO has had this right since forever: Acceptor.openServerSocket
    (SocketServer.scala:657-662) explicitly guards with
    `if (Utils.isBlank(host)) new InetSocketAddress(port)`. The io_uring
    path must mirror that guard exactly, or `auto` is unusable in the
    single most common deployment topology.
    
    Fix
    ===
    Lift Acceptor.openServerSocket's blank-host guard into the io_uring
    bundle construction site. Three lines, no behavior change for any
    non-wildcard listener.
    
    Verification
    ============
    `./gradlew :core:compileScala` succeeds. The targeted io_uring tests
    were unaffected and still pass.
    
    The fix is in core/scala wiring, not in the io_uring transport itself.
    A SocketServer-level integration test for the wildcard io_uring bundle
    would require standing up a live kernel io_uring socket; that gap is
    shared with the rest of the io_uring path (no SocketServer-level test
    exercises it) and is best covered by a separate integration-test pass
    once the v1 scope stabilizes — not by retrofitting a single one-off
    test here.
    
    Co-Authored-By: Claude Opus 4.7 <noreply@anthropic.com>

commit 3e795ba20cd492cabe9f0b0af63537386ef38229
Author: Florent Ramiere <florent@ramiere.com>
Date:   Mon May 18 15:03:54 2026 +0200

    KAFKA-iouring: seed ClientInformation.EMPTY on accept
    
    The NIO Selector seeds ClientInformation.EMPTY into the ChannelMetadataRegistry
    at register-time (clients/.../Selector.java:316-318). The io_uring path created
    its IoUringChannelMetadataRegistry but left clientInformation null, deferring
    initialization until the first ApiVersionsRequest mutates the registry.
    
    This breaks two well-established Kafka assumptions about RequestContext:
    
    1. RequestContext is built BEFORE any ApiVersionsRequest is parsed
       (SocketServer.scala:1201-1215), so the very first request on a connection
       captures whatever ChannelMetadataRegistry.clientInformation() returns at
       that moment. With NIO that is ClientInformation.EMPTY; with io_uring it
       was null.
    
    2. Downstream consumers of context.clientInformation() de-reference without
       a null check:
       - RequestConvertToJson.java:747 calls .softwareName() on the value when
         request logging is at DEBUG, NPE'ing every connection's first request
         under DEBUG.
       - RequestMetrics.java:149-150 calls .softwareName() unconditionally on
         deprecated-API-version requests, NPE'ing those independent of log level.
    
    A malformed first frame still triggers context construction, so the seeding
    must happen on accept — not on first ApiVersionsRequest — to fully mirror NIO.
    
    The new IoUringSelectorTest.acceptInitializesClientInformationToEmpty
    asserts the seeded value on a freshly-accepted io_uring channel.
    
    Codex v12 BLOCKER (verified independently against the cited NPE sites).

    58	/**
    59	 * Broker-side {@link BrokerSelector} backed by Netty's io_uring transport.
    60	 *
    61	 * <h3>What this class does</h3>
    62	 * Owns the per-Processor channel state for an io_uring listener: tracks accepted
    63	 * {@link KafkaChannel} instances, runs the read/write progress loop on {@link #poll(long)},
    64	 * applies idle-connection expiry, and drives mute / unmute semantics. The actual kernel
    65	 * I/O happens on a Netty event-loop thread; this class is driven from the Processor
    66	 * thread and bridged to the event loop through {@link ConcurrentLinkedQueue}s — matching
    67	 * the PROMPT.md acceptance criteria for cross-thread signalling.
    68	 *
    69	 * <h3>What this class does <em>not</em> do</h3>
    70	 * Binding, accepting, and the Netty pipeline live in a separate class (the {@code
    71	 * IoUringServerListener}, follow-up) so this class stays unit-testable without standing
    72	 * up a real socket. The event loop calls into the four package-private hooks:
    73	 * {@link #onAccept(Channel, InetSocketAddress, InetSocketAddress)},
    74	 * {@link #onRead(Channel, ByteBuf)}, {@link #onDisconnect(Channel)}, and
    75	 * {@link #onWritabilityChanged(Channel)}.
    76	 *
    77	 * <h3>Threading invariants</h3>
    78	 * <ul>
    79	 *   <li>Only the Processor thread mutates {@code channels}, {@code lastActiveNanos},
    80	 *       and the four per-poll output collections.</li>
    81	 *   <li>Only the event-loop thread calls the four {@code onX} hooks; those push into
    82	 *       cross-thread queues and release {@link #wakeup}.</li>
    83	 *   <li>{@link #wakeup()}, {@link #send(NetworkSend)}, {@link #mute(String)},
    84	 *       {@link #unmute(String)} are documented as called from the Processor thread per the
    85	 *       {@code Selectable} contract — they do not need to be re-entrant.</li>
    86	 * </ul>
    87	 *
    88	 * <h3>Semantic alignment with NIO Selector</h3>
    89	 * The order of operations inside {@link #poll(long)} is deliberate and mirrors
    90	 * {@code org.apache.kafka.common.network.Selector}:
    91	 * <ol>
    92	 *   <li>Capture {@code nowNanos} once, before any I/O — so a channel that transferred bytes
    93	 *       this tick can never be reaped by idle expiry on the same tick.</li>
    94	 *   <li>Drain accepts first (so a fresh channel can immediately do read/write work).</li>
    95	 *   <li>For each active channel: at most one {@link KafkaChannel#read()} progressing to one
    96	 *       completed receive, then at most one {@link KafkaChannel#write()} progressing to one
    97	 *       completed send. The "one completed receive per channel per poll" cap is a fairness
    98	 *       invariant the request-handling path depends on.</li>
    99	 *   <li>Drain disconnects last — but a channel disconnected mid-tick with buffered bytes
   100	 *       gets one final read pass before it is removed, matching NIO's
   101	 *       {@code closingChannels} semantics. Without this, short-lived clients silently lose
   102	 *       their last request.</li>
   103	 *   <li>Idle expiry sweeps using the {@code nowNanos} captured in step 1.</li>
   104	 *   <li>If no work happened, wait on {@link #wakeup} for the remainder of the timeout.
   105	 *       Permits are drained on acquisition so a burst of event-loop notifications does
   106	 *       not produce N consecutive zero-work polls.</li>
   107	 * </ol>
   108	 */
   109	public final class IoUringSelector implements BrokerSelector {
   110	
   111	    private static final Logger log = LoggerFactory.getLogger(IoUringSelector.class);
   112	
   113	    /** Attached on every Netty channel so the event-loop hooks can route to the right transport. */
   114	    static final AttributeKey<IoUringTransportLayer> TRANSPORT_ATTR =
   115	        AttributeKey.valueOf("kafka.iouring.transport");
   116	    /** Channel id, so {@link #onDisconnect(Channel)} can enqueue it without touching the transport. */
   117	    static final AttributeKey<String> CHANNEL_ID_ATTR =
   118	        AttributeKey.valueOf("kafka.iouring.channelId");
   119	
   120	    private final ListenerName listenerName;
   121	    private final int maxReceiveSize;
   122	    private final MemoryPool memoryPool;
   123	    private final long connectionsMaxIdleNanos;
   124	    private final Time time;
   125	    /**
   126	     * Broker configs threaded down to {@link IoUringPlaintextAuthenticator}'s
   127	     * {@link org.apache.kafka.common.network.ChannelBuilders#createPrincipalBuilder}. Required
   128	     * so a user-configured {@code principal.builder.class} is honored on the io_uring path —
   129	     * passing an empty map would silently fall back to DefaultKafkaPrincipalBuilder, diverging
   130	     * from the NIO PLAINTEXT path on the same broker.
   131	     */
   132	    private final Map<String, ?> configs;
   133	    /**
   134	     * Processor id baked into every connection id we mint. The Kafka request-handling path
   135	     * decrements connection quotas by parsing the connection id with {@link
   136	     * org.apache.kafka.common.network.ServerConnectionId}, so the id must follow the
   137	     * {@code localHost:localPort-remoteHost:remotePort-processorId-index} format — otherwise
   138	     * {@code Processor.processDisconnected} would silently fail to release the quota.
   139	     */
   140	    private final int processorId;
   141	
   142	    // Processor-owned state.
   143	    private final Map<String, KafkaChannel> channels = new LinkedHashMap<>();
   144	    /**
   145	     * Channels that finished serving on this poll but whose final {@code completedReceives}
   146	     * still need a {@code channel(id)} or {@code closingChannel(id)} resolution from the
   147	     * Processor. NIO's {@code KSelector} keeps closing channels alive for one extra poll for
   148	     * exactly this reason — we evict the previous poll's entries at the start of the next
   149	     * poll, after the Processor has had its chance to drain them.
   150	     */
   151	    private final Map<String, KafkaChannel> closingChannels = new HashMap<>();
   152	    /** Crossed by both threads: event loop puts on accept, Processor reads on mute/unmute/close. */
   153	    private final Map<String, Channel> nettyChannels = new ConcurrentHashMap<>();
   154	    private final Map<String, Long> lastActiveNanos = new HashMap<>();
   155	    /**
   156	     * Tracks channels that the operator explicitly muted via {@link #mute(String)} or
   157	     * {@link #muteAll()}, distinct from channels that {@link KafkaChannel#read()} self-muted
   158	     * because {@link MemoryPool#tryAllocate(int)} returned null. Mirrors NIO's
   159	     * {@code Selector.explicitlyMutedChannels} (clients/.../Selector.java:108).
   160	     *
   161	     * <p>The recovery loop at the top of {@link #poll(long)} must NOT unmute channels in this
   162	     * set — they are still muted because the request-handling pipeline (RESPONSE_QUEUED →
   163	     * RESPONSE_SENT mute events) asked us to hold them, not because of memory pressure.
   164	     * Unmuting them would let pipelined requests slip past the throttle.
   165	     *
   166	     * <p>Identity-based set (not id-based) to match NIO's storage; the KafkaChannel instance
   167	     * is stable for the channel's lifetime.
   168	     */
   169	    private final Set<KafkaChannel> explicitlyMutedChannels = new HashSet<>();
   170	    /**
   171	     * Set whenever a channel self-mutes because {@code memoryPool.tryAllocate} returned null
   172	     * during {@link KafkaChannel#read()}. Cleared at the top of the next {@link #poll(long)}
   173	     * when {@code memoryPool.isOutOfMemory()} reports false again — at that point the
   174	     * recovery loop walks every non-explicitly-muted channel and calls {@code maybeUnmute()}.
   175	     *
   176	     * <p>Without this, channels that self-mute under {@code queued.max.bytes} pressure stay
   177	     * MUTED forever (until idle expiry, ~10 min by default) — NIO {@code Selector.poll()}
   178	     * lines 457-466 own this exact recovery path, and the io_uring selector must preserve it.
   179	     */
   180	    private boolean outOfMemory;
   181	
   182	    // Cross-thread queues (event loop pushes, Processor pulls).
   183	    private final Queue<KafkaChannel> pendingAccepts = new ConcurrentLinkedQueue<>();
   184	    /**
   185	     * Cheap O(1) view of {@link #pendingAccepts}'s depth — ConcurrentLinkedQueue.size() walks
   186	     * the chain, so we maintain a counter alongside it. The size is read on every accept to
   187	     * enforce {@link #maxPendingAccepts}; with a high accept rate the O(n) walk would itself
   188	     * become the bottleneck (and would race the producer/consumer pointers).
   189	     */
   190	    private final java.util.concurrent.atomic.AtomicInteger pendingAcceptCount =
   191	        new java.util.concurrent.atomic.AtomicInteger();
   192	    /**
   193	     * Bound for {@link #pendingAccepts}. Mirrors NIO's per-Processor
   194	     * {@code ArrayBlockingQueue(connectionQueueSize)} in {@code SocketServer.Acceptor}:
   195	     * NIO blocks the Acceptor thread on {@code put()} when full, providing kernel-level
   196	     * backpressure via the SYN/ACCEPT backlog. We can't block — the Netty event loop is
   197	     * shared across all child channels for this listener and freezing it would stall every
   198	     * accepted connection — so when the cap is hit, {@link #onAccept(Channel, InetSocketAddress, InetSocketAddress)}
   199	     * closes the freshly accepted netty channel instead. The client sees a RST and the OS
   200	     * backlog drains.
   201	     *
   202	     * <p>The value matches the default for {@code socket.server.listen.backlog.size}'s
   203	     * downstream queue (NIO uses 20 hardcoded per processor). Under sustained pressure the
   204	     * total cap is N * 20 across N selectors, same as NIO.
   205	     */
   206	    private static final int DEFAULT_MAX_PENDING_ACCEPTS = 20;
   207	    private final int maxPendingAccepts;
   208	    private final Queue<String> pendingDisconnects = new ConcurrentLinkedQueue<>();
   209	
   210	    // Wakeup signalling. Permits are drained on acquisition; see acquireWithTimeout below.
   211	    private final Semaphore wakeup = new Semaphore(0);
   212	
   213	    // Per-poll outputs.
   214	    private final List<NetworkReceive> completedReceives = new ArrayList<>();
   215	    private final List<NetworkSend> completedSends = new ArrayList<>();
   216	    private final Map<String, ChannelState> disconnected = new HashMap<>();
   217	    private final List<String> connected = new ArrayList<>();
   218	    /**
   219	     * Channels that just surfaced in {@link #connected} during this poll. The read step
   220	     * (poll step 2) skips them so a newly-accepted channel cannot produce a
   221	     * {@code completedReceive} in the same poll where it first appears to the Processor —
   222	     * otherwise SocketServer.applyConnectionQuotasForNewlyAcceptedChannels (which runs
   223	     * AFTER poll and may refuse the channel via {@code selector.close}) would leave a
   224	     * receive in {@link #completedReceives} pointing at a channel already removed, and
   225	     * Processor.processCompletedReceives would (a) emit the request to the request queue
   226	     * before the refusal completes, and (b) trip an IllegalStateException whose cleanup
   227	     * path calls {@code connectionQuotas.dec} on a connection that was never {@code inc}'d.
   228	     */
   229	    private final Set<String> justAccepted = new HashSet<>();
   230	    /**
   231	     * Channel ids that produced a {@link NetworkReceive} in this poll. The
   232	     * "one completed receive per channel per poll" invariant — documented in the class
   233	     * javadoc and enforced by NIO's {@code Selector.addToCompletedReceives} via an
   234	     * {@code IllegalStateException} on duplicate inserts — needs to hold across the
   235	     * step-2 read pass <em>and</em> the step-3 disconnect drain. If both fire for the
   236	     * same channel id (peer FIN arrives mid-poll, with another pipelined frame still
   237	     * buffered), the request channel would otherwise see two requests for one
   238	     * connection and the KafkaChannel mute-after-receive contract breaks.
   239	     */
   240	    private final Set<String> receivesThisPoll = new HashSet<>();
   241	    /**
   242	     * Channels for which {@link #send(NetworkSend)} could not deliver because the peer
   243	     * had already disconnected (id is in {@link #closingChannels}) or {@link
   244	     * KafkaChannel#setSend} threw. Drained at the start of the next poll into
   245	     * {@link #disconnected} with {@link ChannelState#FAILED_SEND}, mirroring NIO's
   246	     * {@code Selector.failedSends} pipeline so the Processor sees a normal disconnect
   247	     * notification rather than an {@code IllegalStateException} that would otherwise
   248	     * propagate up through {@code Processor.processChannelException} and double-dec the
   249	     * connection quota.
   250	     */
   251	    private final List<String> failedSends = new ArrayList<>();
   252	
   253	    // 32-bit wrapping counter, NOT AtomicLong. ServerConnectionId.fromString parses
   254	    // the index segment via Integer.parseInt, so a long that exceeds Integer.MAX_VALUE
   255	    // (~2.1B accepts on a long-running broker — reachable under connection-churn) would
   256	    // make fromString return Optional.empty, defeating SocketServer.processDisconnected
   257	    // and leaking connection-quota slots until restart. NIO uses an int that wraps at
   258	    // Int.MaxValue (SocketServer.scala line 1431-1432); we mirror that wrap exactly.
   259	    private final AtomicInteger idGen = new AtomicInteger();
   260	    private volatile boolean closed;

   262	    /** Test-only constructor: uses processor id 0 and an empty configs map (default principal builder). */
   263	    public IoUringSelector(ListenerName listenerName,
   264	                           int maxReceiveSize,
   265	                           MemoryPool memoryPool,
